Convergence to the equilibrium for the kinetic transport equation in the two-dimensional periodic Lorentz Gas Thanks: This work has been partially supported by the grant "Progetti di ricerca d’Ateneo 2024" by Sapienza University, Rome and by GNFM - INdAM. Co-funded by the European Union (ERC CoG KiLiM, project number 101125162).
Francesca Pieroni 1

Abstract
. We consider the kinetic transport equation that arise in the Boltzmann-Grad limit of the two-dimensional periodic Lorentz Gas. This equation has been obtained by extending the phase space of positions and velocities through the introduction of two new variables, representing respectively the time to the next collision and the corresponding impact parameter. Here we mostly focus on the case of periodic boundary conditions on the positions space: we prove that, under suitable hypothesis, the time evolution of a probability density on the extended phase space converges to the equilibrium state with respect to the L p L^{p} norm ( ∗ -weakly if p = ∞ p=\infty ), if such initial density is L p L^{p} . If p = 2 p=2 , or if the initial datum does not depend on the position, we also get more precise estimates about the rate of the approach to the equilibrium. Our proof is based on the analysis of the long time behavior of the Fourier coefficients of the solution.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
35Q20, 82B40

1.
Introduction

The Lorentz Gas is the dynamical system describing the motion of a classical particle interacting through elastic collisions with a system of obstacles. We will focus here on hard-core scatterers with spherical symmetry, and since the obstacles are infinitely heavy, they do not move. This model was first proposed by Lorentz
[
13
]
to describe the motion of the electrons in a metal.

Let the spherical obstacles with centers
c:=\{c_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and radius
\varepsilon
. Then, the motion of the particle is described as follows. If no collisions occur, that is, as long as the distance between the particle and any obstacle is larger than
\varepsilon
, the particle’s motion is free: given
(x,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
|x-c_{j}|>\varepsilon\quad\forall j\in\mathbb{N}
, and denoting by
(X_{t},V_{t})
the position and velocity of the particle at time
t
, the dynamics is described by

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcr}X_{0}(x,v;c)=x,\dot{X}_{t}(x,v;c)=V_{t}(x,v;c),\\
V_{0}(x,v;c)=v,\dot{V}_{t}(x,v;c)=0,\end{array}\right.\hskip 9.24994pt\textrm{ if }|X_{t}(x,v;c)-c_{j}|>\varepsilon\hskip 9.24994pt\forall j\in\mathbb{N},

but when the particle hits an obstacle, it gets specularly reflected. That is, if the particle collides at time
t
with the obstacle
\bar{j}
, then
|X_{t}(x,v;c)-c_{\bar{j}}|=\varepsilon
and

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcr}X_{t^{+}}(x,v;\{c_{j}\}_{j})=X_{t^{-}}(x,v;c),\\
V_{t^{+}}(x,v;c)={\mathcal{R}}\left[\frac{X_{t}(x,v;c)-c_{\bar{j}}}{\varepsilon}\right]V_{t^{-}}(x,v;c),\end{array}\right.

where
f_{t^{\pm}}
denote respectively
\lim_{s\to t^{\pm}}f(s)
and
{\mathcal{R}}[x]v
is the orthogonal reflection of
v
with respect to the real line of direction
x
, i.e.
{\mathcal{R}}[x]v=-v_{x}+v_{x}^{\perp}
when
v=v_{x}+v_{x}^{\perp},v_{x}\in\mathbb{R}x,v_{x}^{\perp}\in(\mathbb{R}x)^{\perp}
.

The obstacles may overlap, that is, it could happen that, for
j\neq k\in\mathbb{N}
,
0<|c_{j}-c_{k}|\leq 2\varepsilon
. In this case, the dynamics is not well defined only if the particles hits both the obstacles at the same time, i.e, if
|X_{t}(x,v;c)-c_{j}|=|X_{t}(x,v;c)-c_{k}|=\varepsilon
, since the obstacles should reflect the velocity vector in two different directions. The points that belong to the boundary of two different obstacles are usually referred as "angular points". This problem is overcome by noticing that the Lebesgue measure of the set of initial data
(x,v)
such that the particle hits an angular point is zero (see for example
[
2
]
).

Since
|v|
is preserved within the motion, it is assumed to be 1 with no loss of generality.

Therefore if one considers a particle with randomly distributed initial data
(x,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, for example through a density function
\mu_{in}(x,v)
, for finite
\varepsilon>0
the time evolution of the density is

\mu_{t}(x,v;c)=\mu_{in}(X_{-t}(x,v;c),V_{-t}(x,v;c)),

with
X_{t}
and
V_{t}
described in (
1
) and (
1
), and one may ask under what assumptions on
c
the quantity
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mu_{t}(x,v;c)
exists and is non trivial. Typically, one should scale also the time
t
(i.e., dividing it by
\varepsilon
).

Here we are focusing on the low density case.

The case of low density and randomly Poisson distributed obstacles was first studied by Gallavotti
[
9
]
. The author proved that if a point particle moves in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and the centers of the obstacles are a Poisson point process of intensity
n\sim\frac{1}{2\lambda\varepsilon}
, then for any continuous and bounded probability density
\mu_{in}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\to[0,+\infty)
there exists the limit of the averaged particle density

(1.0.5)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mu_{t}(\cdot,\cdot;c)]\xrightarrow[\varepsilon\to 0]{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{S}^{1})}\mu_{t},\textrm{ uniformly on compact }t\textrm{-sets},

where the expected value is taken with respect to the Poisson distribution of the obstacles’ centers
c
.

The previous limit is the low-density limit, that is, the Boltzmann-Grad limit, that is, one lets the size of the scatterer radius
\varepsilon\to 0
but keeping constant the mean free path length. The mean free path length is
\lambda\sim(2n\varepsilon)^{-1}
.

Moreover the limiting
\mu_{t}
satisfies the Boltzmann equation

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcr}\partial_{t}\mu_{t}(x,v)+v\cdot\nabla_{x}\mu_{t}(x,v)+\lambda\mu_{t}(x,v)=\frac{\lambda}{4}\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\theta\mu_{t}(x,{\mathcal{R}}[\theta]v)\sin(\frac{\theta}{2}),\\
\mu_{0}(x,v)=\mu_{in}(x,v),\end{array}\right.

where
{\mathcal{R}}[\theta]v
is the rotation of
v
by angle
\pi-\theta
, i.e., the same rotation
{\mathcal{R}}
as before expressed as a function of the angle of the impact.

Spohn
[
21
]
strengthened the previous result by proving that the convergence of the Lorentz process

(X_{t}(x,v;c),V_{t}(x,v;c))

to a stochastic process holds with respect to the weak
∗
topology of regular Borel measures on the paths space, even if more general random distributions of the obstacles are taken into account.

A further related result was obtained by Boldrighini-Bunimovich-Sinai
[
2
]
: the authors proved that the scaled Lorentz process converges for almost all
c
configurations of the obstacles’ centers.

Then a natural question is what changes in (
1.0.5
) and in (
1
) if the obstacles’ centers have periodic configuration, instead of being randomly distributed, and the expectation sign in (
1.0.5
) is transferred to the initial data. This model is better known as periodic Lorentz Gas and it has been studied by several authors.

1.1.
The periodic Lorentz Gas.

From now on, in this introduction we will assume
0<\varepsilon<\frac{1}{2}
, the obstacles’ centers
c
to be located in
\varepsilon\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and to have radius
r_{\varepsilon}:=\varepsilon^{\frac{d}{d-1}}
. Therefore the available region where a particle can move is

(1.1.1)

\displaystyle Z_{\varepsilon}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\textrm{dist(}x,\varepsilon\mathbb{Z}^{d}\textrm{)}\geq\varepsilon^{\frac{d}{d-1}}\}.

This way, the free path length of a particle moving in
Z_{\varepsilon}
according to (
1
) and (
1
) can be defined as

\tau_{\varepsilon}(x,v):=\inf\{t>0:x+tv\in\partial Z_{\varepsilon}\},\qquad(x,v)\in Z_{\varepsilon}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}.

The setting where the gas is to be studied for fixed time
t>0
is exactly (
1.1.1
), indeed this condition ensures that the obstacles’ density is
\simeq 1/\varepsilon^{d}
. Therefore, since a circular tube of radius
\varepsilon^{\frac{d}{d-1}}
, that is the obstacles’ radius, around the line drawn by trajectory in the time interval
[0,t]
has volume
\simeq(\varepsilon^{\frac{d}{d-1}})^{d-1}t=t\varepsilon^{d}
, the mean obstacles number hit before time
t
is
\simeq t\varepsilon^{d}\cdot 1/(\varepsilon^{d})=t
. Thus, in this setting the mean free path length should have order 1 as
\varepsilon\to 0
(see for example Dumas-Dumas-Golse
[
8
]
and Golse
[
11
]
). Nevertheless, an equivalent and perhaps more common setting considered in literature involves placing the obstacles in
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, instead of
\varepsilon\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, making them have radius
\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{d-1}}
, instead of
\varepsilon^{\frac{d}{d-1}}
, and studying the dynamics at times
t/\varepsilon
, instead of
t
.

1.1.1.
The distribution of the free path length in the Boltzmann-Grad limit.

In the Boltzmann-Grad limit of the periodic Lorentz gas, Bourgain-Golse-Wennberg
[
3
]
and Golse-Wennberg
[
12
]
proved that, if one denotes by
A/B
the quotient of
A
with respect to the equivalence relation
x\sim y\Leftrightarrow x-y\in B
, being
\nu_{\varepsilon}
is the uniform probability measure on
Z_{\varepsilon}/(\varepsilon\mathbb{Z}^{d})\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, then

(1.1.2)

\displaystyle\Phi_{\varepsilon}(t):=\nu_{\varepsilon}\{(x,v)\in Z_{\varepsilon}/(\varepsilon\mathbb{Z}^{d})\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}:\tau_{\varepsilon}(x,v)>t\}\simeq\frac{1}{t^{d-1}},\quad\forall t\geq 1,

where
f\simeq g
means that there exists a constant
C>0
such that
C^{-1}g\leq f\leq Cg
, and such a constant in the above inequality does not depend on
\varepsilon
. In other words,
\Phi_{\varepsilon}
is the probability that the free path length is larger than
t
: in dimension
d=2
, (
1.1.2
) makes clear that the mean free path length is not finite if the average is computed with respect to
\nu_{\varepsilon}
. Instead, if one substitutes
\nu_{\varepsilon}
with another probability measure concentrated on the boundary of the obstacles (see
[
8
]
), the mean free path length is finite.

In dimension
d=2
, a result related to (
1.1.2
) was obtained by Caglioti-Golse
[
5
]
, indeed the authors provided the exact behavior of the limit for large
t
, as
\varepsilon\to 0
, of
\Phi_{\varepsilon}
, i.e.

\lim_{t\to+\infty}t\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{|\log\varepsilon|}\int_{\varepsilon}^{\frac{1}{4}}\frac{dr}{r}\Phi_{r}(t)=\frac{2}{\pi^{2}}.

Boca-Zaharescu
[
1
]
strenghtened their method obtaining exact estimates for

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\Phi_{\varepsilon}(t),

that is, the limiting distribution of the free path length, at any fixed time
t\geq 0
.

Then, Caglioti-Golse
[
6
,
7
]
, Marklof-Strömbergsson
[
15
]
and Bykovskii-Ustinovin
[
4
]
found an explicit expression for the transition kernel
Q
in dimension
d=2
, that is, the Boltzmann-Grad limit of the infinitesimal probability that the next obstacle will be hit in time
s
and with impact parameter
h
, conditioning the impact parameter of the previous collision to be
h^{\prime}
. The impact parameter
h
is defined for finite
\varepsilon>0
as
h:=\sin(\widehat{v^{\prime}n_{x}})
, with
v^{\prime}
the velocity after the next collision and
n_{x}
the exterior normal to the obstacle in the impact site
x
. This way, the limiting probability that the next obstacle will be hit in time
t
writes as a function of the transition kernel
Q
. Such transition kernel
Q
is

Definition 1.1
.

\displaystyle Q(s,h|h^{\prime}):=\frac{6}{\pi^{2}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcr}1&&0\leq s\leq\frac{1}{1+h},\\
\frac{\frac{1}{s}-(1+h^{\prime})}{h-h^{\prime}}&&\frac{1}{1+h}<s\leq\frac{1}{1+h^{\prime}},\\
0&&s<0\textrm{ or }s>\frac{1}{1+h^{\prime}},\end{array}\right.\quad\textrm{ if }|h^{\prime}|\leq h,

and the definition extends to all
h,h^{\prime}\in[-1,1]
by using the symmetries

\displaystyle Q(s,h|h^{\prime})=Q(s,h^{\prime}|h)=Q(s,-h|-h^{\prime}).

The stated results about the structure of the transition probability
Q
hold for
(x,v)
randomly distributed on
Z_{\varepsilon}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
with a probability density absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure instead of being uniformly distributed on
Z_{\varepsilon}/(\varepsilon\mathbb{Z}^{2})\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
. Moreover, in
[
16
]
the authors also provided a definition for the impact parameter
h
and for the kernel
Q
for dimensions
d\geq 3
: their results involve all the dimensions
d\geq 2
but
Q
has not an explicit formulation for all the times
s
for dimensions
d\neq 2
.

1.1.2.
The kinetic theory for the periodic Lorentz gas in the Boltzmann-Grad limit.

Going back to (
1
), one may also ask whether it is possible to obtain such a kinetic equation in the periodic case. The answer is no: using the heavy tail of the distribution of the free path length, Golse
[
10
]
proved that in any dimension
d\geq 2
there exists an initial datum
\mu_{in}:\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\to[0,+\infty)
such that if the obstacles are balls of radius
\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{d-1}}
located in
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, no subsequence of the density
\{\mu_{\frac{t}{\varepsilon}}\}_{\varepsilon}
with initial datum
\mu_{in}
can converge to the solution of the linear Boltzmann equation.

In
[
6
,
7
]
the authors obtained an equation, rigorously derived in
[
14
]
, for the time evolution of the limiting density. We restate it in dimension
d=2
, even if in
[
14
]
any dimension
d\geq 2
has been considered. Denoting by
\mu_{in}
the limiting probability density of the initial data, the equation writes as

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcr}\frac{\partial\mu_{t}}{\partial t}(x,v,s,h)+v\cdot\nabla_{x}\mu_{t}(x,v,s,h)-\frac{\partial\mu_{t}}{\partial s}(x,v,s,h)=\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}Q(s,h|h^{\prime})\mu_{t}(x,{\mathcal{R}}[\theta(h^{\prime})]v,0,h^{\prime}),\\
\mu_{t}(x,v,s,h)|_{t=0}=\mu_{in}(x,v)E(s,h).\end{array}\right.

Let us comment the above expression. The key argument used by the authors to understand the time evolution of the Boltzmann-Grad limit of a probability density on the phase space was to extend the phase space itself by adding the couple
(s,h)=
(time to the next collision, impact parameter of the next collision) defined before. Indeed the random flight
(X_{t},V_{t})_{t\geq 0}
in the Boltzmann-Grad limit obtained in
[
14
]
is not a Markov process (in
(x,v)
), therefore it is not possible to find a memoryless equation for the limiting density
\mu_{t}(x,v)
. Thus, the probability measure on the new expanded phase space
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\times[0,+\infty)\times[-1,1]
is to be understood as the probability of having at time
t
position
x
and velocity
v
, hitting the next obstacle within time
s
, i.e., at time
t+s
, and with impact parameter
h
.

There are still some quantities to be commented in (
1.1.2
). One of them is
E
, that is, the invariant probability measure for the time evolution of the density in
(s,h)
, obtained as

Definition 1.2
.

\displaystyle E(s,h):=\int_{s}^{\infty}ds^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}Q(s^{\prime},h|h^{\prime}),

while
{\mathcal{R}}[\theta(h^{\prime})]
rotates a vector
v=(\cos\theta,\sin\theta)\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
by angle
\theta(h^{\prime})=\pi-2\arcsin(h^{\prime})
, that is

{\mathcal{R}}[\theta(h^{\prime})]v=(\cos(\theta+\theta(h^{\prime})),\sin(\theta+\theta(h^{\prime}))).

Let us finally point out that in the previous equation
x
can be a point in
\mathbb{T}^{2}
or in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. This ambiguity comes from the obstacles’ lattice being periodic.

The time evolution equation (
1.1.2
) raises the problem of studying the long time behavior of the solution
\mu_{t}
. To begin with, we notice that one can introduce randomness also in the new parameters
(s,h)
, getting thus the following equation for the time evolution of a density

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcr}\frac{\partial\mu_{t}}{\partial t}(x,v,s,h)+v\cdot\nabla_{x}\mu_{t}(x,v,s,h)-\frac{\partial\mu_{t}}{\partial s}(x,v,s,h)=\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}Q(s,h|h^{\prime})\mu_{t}(x,{\mathcal{R}}[\theta(h^{\prime})]v,0,h^{\prime}),\\
\mu_{t}(x,v,s,h)|_{t=0}=\mu_{0}(x,v,s,h).\end{array}\right.

For our purpose using
\theta\in\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}:=\mathbb{R}/(2\pi\mathbb{Z})
as a parameter is more comfortable rather than
v\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
, therefore hereafter we will denote

\mu(x,\theta,s,h):=\mu(x,v(\theta),s,h)\quad\textrm{ if }\quad v(\theta)=(\cos\theta,\sin\theta),

and writing
\mu_{t}
as a function of
\theta
instead of
v
, the equation (
1.1.2
) writes as

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcr}\frac{\partial\mu_{t}}{\partial t}(x,\theta,s,h)+v(\theta)\cdot\nabla_{x}\mu_{t}(x,\theta,s,h)-\frac{\partial\mu_{t}}{\partial s}(x,\theta,s,h)=\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}Q(s,h|h^{\prime})\mu_{t}(x,\theta+\pi-2\arcsin(h^{\prime}),0,h^{\prime}),\\
\mu_{t}(x,\theta,s,h)|_{t=0}=\mu_{0}(x,\theta,s,h).\end{array}\right.

In
[
14
]
existence and uniqueness for the solutions of (
1.1.2
) have been proved, as well as the fact that the
L^{1}
distance between two solutions is non increasing in time. As pointed out by the authors, a solution of (
1.1.2
) can be represented by

\displaystyle\mu_{t}(x,\theta,s,h)

\displaystyle=\mu_{0}(x-tv(\theta),\theta,s+t,h)

(1.1.9)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}Q(s+t-t^{\prime},h|h^{\prime})\mu_{t^{\prime}}(x-(t-t^{\prime})v(\theta),\theta+\pi-2\arcsin(h^{\prime}),0,h^{\prime}),

so that the solution turns out to be the sum of two contributions: the first one can be understood as the probability density of the particles that collide for the first time at time
t
, while the second one represents the probability density of the particles that have collided at least one time before time
t
. Moreover, if we evaluate the equation (
1.1.9
) at
s=0
we get

\displaystyle\mu_{t}(x,\theta,0,h)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\mu_{0}(x-tv(\theta),\theta,t,h)}_{=:\tilde{\mu}_{0}(x,\theta,t,h)}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}Q(t-t^{\prime},h|h^{\prime})\mu_{t^{\prime}}(x-(t-t^{\prime})v(\theta),\theta+\pi-2\arcsin(h^{\prime}),0,h^{\prime})}_{=:{\mathcal{F}}(\mu)(x,\theta,t,h)},

and, except for the initial datum
\mu_{0}
, this equation involves only
\mu_{t}(x,\theta,s=0,h)
, that is, the probability density of the particles that collide at time
t
.

The first contribution
\tilde{\mu}_{0}
can be understood again as the probability density of the particles whose first collision occurs at time
t
, while the second contribution
{\mathcal{F}}(\mu)(t,x,\theta,h)
represents the probability density of the particles that have collided at least one time before time
t
.

Since
{\mathcal{F}}
is a linear function of
\mu
, we can formally write
\mu_{t}(x,\theta,0,h)
as

\mu_{t}(x,\theta,0,h)=\tilde{\mu}_{0}(x,\theta,t,h)+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{\mathcal{F}}^{n}(\tilde{\mu}_{0})(x,\theta,t,h),

where each term
{\mathcal{F}}^{n}(\tilde{\mu}_{0})(x,\theta,t,h)
represents the probability density of the particles that have collided exactly
n
times before time
t
. Going back to (
1.1.9
), the advantage of such a representation is that it makes sense not only for regular initial data but for general
L^{1}
functions.

In the previous equation four variables are involved:
(x,\theta,s,h)
, but by integrating
\mu_{t}
with respect to
x
or
(x,\theta)
, one gets two new equations in the remaining variables.

Averaging on the position
x
.
By integrating
\mu_{t}
with respect to
x
one gets

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcr}\frac{\partial\mu_{t}}{\partial t}(\theta,s,h)-\frac{\partial\mu_{t}}{\partial s}(\theta,s,h)=\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}Q(s,h|h^{\prime})\mu_{t}(\theta+\pi-2\arcsin(h^{\prime}),0,h^{\prime}),\\
\mu_{t}|_{t=0}(\theta,s,h)=\mu_{0}(\theta,s,h).\end{array}\right.

The same equation would appear by considering an initial datum that does not depend on
x
, but of course this makes sense only for
x\in\mathbb{T}^{2}
and not for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. That is, if
\mu_{0}
does not depend on
x
, the same holds for
\mu_{t}
at any time
t
. Of course, a solution of (
1.1.2
) admits also an alternative representation which does not include derivatives, that is,

(1.1.12)

\displaystyle\mu_{t}(\theta,s,h)=\mu_{0}(\theta,s+t,h)+\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}Q(s+t-t^{\prime},h|h^{\prime})\mu_{t^{\prime}}(\theta+\pi-2\arcsin(h^{\prime}),0,h^{\prime}),\hskip 8.19447pt\forall t\geq 0,

with the same interpretation about the number of collision within time
t
as before.

Averaging on both the position
x
and the velocity
v(\theta)
.
Instead if one integrates both respect to
x
and to
v
, the final equation would be

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcr}\frac{\partial\mu_{t}}{\partial t}(s,h)-\frac{\partial\mu_{t}}{\partial s}(s,h)=\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}Q(s,h|h^{\prime})\mu_{t}(0,h^{\prime}),\\
\mu_{t}|_{t=0}(s,h)=\mu_{0}(s,h),\end{array}\right.

also written for more general
L^{1}
initial data as

(1.1.15)

\displaystyle\mu_{t}(s,h)=\mu_{0}(s+t,h)+\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}Q(s+t-t^{\prime},h|h^{\prime})\mu_{t^{\prime}}(0,h^{\prime}),\qquad\forall t\geq 0.

One can notice again that if the initial datum
\mu_{0}
does not depend on
x
or on
(x,\theta)
, neither does
\mu_{t}
at any time
t
, and therefore the evolution of the probability density with respect to the remaining variables
(s,h)
is described by the equation (
1.1.2
) (respectively (
1.1.2
)). In
[
7
,
14
]
it has been proven that the only equilibria states for the equation (
1.1.2
) in
(s,h)
are
cE
, for a constant
c\in\mathbb{R}
and
E\in L^{1}([0,+\infty)\times[-1,1])
introduced in Definition
1.2
.

Moreover, since we already observed that a generalized solution of the three kinetic equations above exists also for non regular initial data, we will use the following notation.

Definition 1.3
.

A mild solution of equation (
1.1.2
) (respectively (
1.1.2
) and (
1.1.2
)) with initial datum
\mu_{0}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}\times[0,+\infty)\times[-1,1])
or
\mu_{0}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}\times[0,+\infty)\times[-1,1])
(respectively
\mu_{0}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}\times[0,+\infty)\times[-1,1])
and
\mu_{0}\in L^{1}([0,+\infty)\times[-1,1])
) is a function that satisties (
1.1.9
) (respectively (
1.1.12
) and (
1.1.15
)).

1.1.3.
Further literature.

Marklof-Strömbergsson
[
17
]
also provided asymptotic estimates, that is, for small and large
s
, for the kernel
Q
and the invariant measure
E
in any dimension
d\geq 2
. These bounds rely on the explicit formulation of the transition kernel
Q
only in dimension
d=2
and allow to improve the estimates about the distribution of the free path length in (
1.1.2
). In
[
18
]
the authors also generalized these asymptotic estimates and the time evolution law (
1.1.2
) to the case of finite unions of lattices, while in
[
19
]
also the case of spherically symmetric finite-range potentials has been studied. Moreover Marklof-Tóth
[
20
]
proved a superdiffusive limit with normalization factor
\sqrt{t\log t}
, instead of
\sqrt{t}
, for the continuous and discrete time displacement in any dimension
d\geq 2
.

1.2.
Main results.

The main results we prove concern the asymptotic behavior of the mild solutions of the equations (
1.1.2
), (
1.1.2
) and (
1.1.2
). In
[
7
]
, using relative entropy estimates, it has been proved that if
\mu_{in}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi})
is a probability density, then the time evolution of
\mu_{0}:=\mu_{in}E
converges to the equilibrium state

(1.2.1)

\displaystyle\mu_{t}\xrightarrow[t\to+\infty]{}\frac{1}{2\pi}E,\quad\textrm{weak}-^{*}\textrm{ in }L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}\times[0,+\infty)\times[-1,1]),

and also that the rate of the approach to the equilibrium with respect to the
L^{2}
norm is worse than
t^{-\frac{3}{2}}
.

Our purpose is to improve this result including also some estimates on the rate of convergence to the equilibrium.

Hereafter we will denote by
\langle f\rangle
the integral of
f
.

Our first result is the following.

Theorem 1.1
.

There exists a constant
C>0
depending only on
Q
such that for any
p\in[1,+\infty]
and
\mu_{0}\in L^{1}\cap L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}\times[0,+\infty)\times[-1,1])
, if
\mu_{t}
is the mild solution of the equation (
1.1.2
) with initial datum
\mu_{0}
, then

\displaystyle\left\|\mu_{t}-\frac{\langle\mu_{0}\rangle}{2\pi}E\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}\times[0,+\infty)\times[-1,1])}

\displaystyle\leq C\frac{\left\|\mu_{0}\right\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}\times[0,+\infty)\times[-1,1])}+\left\|\mu_{0}\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}\times[0,+\infty)\times[-1,1])}}{t+1}

(1.2.2)

\displaystyle+C\left[\left\|\mu_{0}\right\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}\times[t/4,+\infty)\times[-1,1])}+\left\|\mu_{0}\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}\times[t/4,+\infty)\times[-1,1])}\right].

In particular, if
\mu_{0}(\theta,s,h)=\mu_{in}(\theta)E(s,h)
with
\mu_{in}\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi})
, then
\mu_{0}\in L^{1}\cap L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}\times[0,+\infty)\times[-1,1])
and it holds

(1.2.3)

\displaystyle\left\|\mu_{t}-\frac{\langle\mu_{in}\rangle}{2\pi}E\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}\times[0,+\infty)\times[-1,1])}\leq\frac{C}{t+1}\left\|\mu_{in}\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi})}.

Notice that if
\mu_{0}
does not depend on
\theta
neither
\mu_{t}
does at any time
t
, thus Theorem
1.1
includes also the mild solutions of (
1.1.2
). We also point out that if
p
is finite, (
1.2.2
) of the previous Theorem
1.1
states that the left-hand side of the inequality vanishes as
t\to+\infty
. This holds also for
p=\infty
only with the further assumption that
\|\mu_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}\times[t,+\infty)\times[-1,1])}\xrightarrow[t\to+\infty]{}0
, such as, for example, if
\mu_{0}(\theta,s,h)=\mu^{in}(\theta)E(s,h)
, as in the statement (
1.2.3
).

To state the second result we have to introduce the Fourier coefficients of a mild solution of (
1.1.2
), defined both for a mild solution of the equation with
x\in\mathbb{T}^{2}
and with
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
respectively as

(1.2.4)

\displaystyle\mu_{t}^{k}(\theta,s,h):=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}dxe^{2\pi ik\cdot x}\mu_{t}(x,\theta,s,h),\hskip 8.19447ptk\in\mathbb{Z}^{2};\hskip 8.19447pt\mu_{t}^{k}(\theta,s,h):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}dxe^{2\pi ik\cdot x}\mu_{t}(x,\theta,s,h),\hskip 8.19447ptk\in\mathbb{R}^{2}.

We shall prove the following result about the Fourier coefficients above.

Theorem 1.2
.

There exists a constant
C>0
depending only on
Q
such that for any
p\in[1,+\infty]
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2},k\neq(0,0)
, if
\mu_{0}\in L^{1}\cap L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}\times[0,+\infty)\times[-1,1])
and
\{\mu_{t}^{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
are the Fourier coefficients (
1.2.4
) of the mild solution of equation (
1.1.2
) with initial datum
\mu_{0}
, then

\displaystyle\left\|\mu_{t}^{k}\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}\times[0,+\infty)\times[-1,1])}

\displaystyle\leq C\frac{\|\mu_{0}^{k}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}\times[0,+\infty)\times[-1,1])}+\|\mu_{0}^{k}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}\times[0,+\infty)\times[-1,1])}}{t+1}

(1.2.5)

\displaystyle+C\left[\|\mu_{0}^{k}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}\times[\frac{t}{4},+\infty)\times[-1,1])}+\|\mu_{0}^{k}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}\times[\frac{t}{4},+\infty)\times[-1,1])}\right],

and in particular, if
\mu_{0}(x,\theta,s,h)=\mu_{in}(x,\theta)E(s,h)
with
\mu_{in}\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi})
, then
\mu_{0}\in L^{1}\cap L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}\times[0,+\infty)\times[-1,1])
and it holds

(1.2.6)

\displaystyle\left\|\mu_{t}^{k}\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}\times[0,+\infty)\times[-1,1])}\leq\frac{C}{t+1}\|\mu_{in}^{k}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi})}.

Up to substituting the constant
C
with
\frac{C}{\min\{1,|k|^{6}\}}
, the same estimates hold for
p=1
, initial datum
\mu_{0}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}\times[0,+\infty)\times[-1,1])
and Fourier coefficients
\{\mu_{t}^{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{(0,0)\}}
.

As we commented before about Theorem
1.1
, also in this case we can notice that, in the first statement (
1.2.5
) of Theorem
1.2
, the left-hand side of the inequality is vanishing for
p\in[1,+\infty)
, but it is not necessarily infinitesimal if
p=\infty
. It is under the further assumption that also
\|\mu_{0}^{k}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}\times[t,+\infty)\times[-1,1])}
vanishes as
t\to+\infty
. That is, for example, the case of initial data
\mu_{0}(x,\theta,s,h)=\mu_{in}(x,\theta)E(s,h)
, as stated in (
1.2.6
).

If combined with Proposition
3.1
in Section
3
, Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
imply the following result on the flat torus
\mathbb{T}^{2}
.

Theorem 1.3
.

Fix
p\in[1,+\infty)
, let
\mu_{0}\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}\times[0,+\infty)\times[-1,1])
such that

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}}d\theta\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\int_{-1}^{1}dh\|\mu_{0}(\cdot,\theta,s,h)\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}<+\infty,

and let
\mu_{t}
be the mild solution of (
1.1.2
) with initial datum
\mu_{0}
. Then
\mu_{0}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}\times[0,+\infty)\times[-1,1])
and it holds

(1.2.7)

\displaystyle\left\|\mu_{t}-\frac{\langle\mu_{0}\rangle}{2\pi}E\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}\times[0,+\infty)\times[-1,1])}\xrightarrow[t\to+\infty]{}0.

Under the same conditions, if
p=\infty

(1.2.8)

\displaystyle\mu_{t}\xrightarrow[t\to+\infty]{L^{\infty}*\textrm{-weakly}}\frac{\langle\mu_{0}\rangle}{2\pi}E.

Moreover there exists a constant
C>0
such that for any
\mu_{0}
satisfying the hypothesis above for
p=2
it holds

\displaystyle\left\|\mu_{t}-\frac{\langle\mu_{0}\rangle}{2\pi}E\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}\times[0,+\infty)\times[-1,1])}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{t+1}\|\mu_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}\times[0,+\infty)\times[-1,1])}

\displaystyle+\frac{C}{t+1}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}}d\theta\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\int_{-1}^{1}dh\|\mu_{0}(\cdot,\theta,s,h)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}

\displaystyle+C\|\mu_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}\times[\frac{t}{4},+\infty)\times[-1,1])}

(1.2.9)

\displaystyle+C\int_{\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}}d\theta\int_{\frac{t}{4}}^{\infty}ds\int_{-1}^{1}dh\|\mu_{0}(\cdot,\theta,s,h)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})},

and, in particular, if
\mu_{0}(x,\theta,s,h)=\mu_{in}(x,\theta)E(s,h)
with
\mu_{in}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi})
, it holds

(1.2.10)

\displaystyle\left\|\mu_{t}-\frac{\langle\mu_{in}\rangle}{2\pi}E\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}\times[0,+\infty)\times[-1,1])}\leq\frac{C}{t+1}\|\mu_{in}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi})}.

The two hypothesis on
\mu_{0}
in the previous Theorem, actually coincident if
p=1
, are exactly the hypothesis that ensure that
\{\mu_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
is bounded in
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}\times[0,+\infty)\times[-1,1])
(see (
3.0.5
) of Proposition
3.1
). These conditions cover, for example, the cases
\mu_{0}(x,\theta,s,h)=\mu_{in}(x,\theta)E(s,h)
, with
\mu_{in}\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi})
, as well as any
\mu_{0}(x,\theta,s,h)=\mu_{in}(x)\nu_{0}(\theta,s,h)
,
\mu_{in}(x,\theta)\nu_{0}(s,h)
,
\mu_{in}(x,\theta,h)\nu_{0}(s)
with
\mu_{in}\in L^{p}
and
\nu_{0}\in L^{1}\cap L^{p}
on the respective spaces.

The
L^{2}
norm is the only one that we can use to get quantitative estimates about the rate of the approach to the equilibrium because the results are achieved by studying the long time behavior of the Fourier coefficients, as stated in Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
.

We also point out that (
1.2.8
) extends the result (
1.2.1
) in
[
7
]
to a slightly more general class of initial data, and also that (
1.2.9
) complies with the negative result in
[
7
]
we mentioned before, according to which the rate of the approach to the equilibrium with respect to the
L^{2}
norm should be worse than
t^{-\frac{3}{2}}
, for given initial data
\mu_{in}(x,\theta)E(s,h)
,
\mu_{in}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi})
.

Lastly, we prove the following result concerning the mild solutions of equation (
1.1.2
) for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Before that, notice that if
\mu_{t}
is a mild solution defined on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, the previous results on the flat torus
\mathbb{T}^{2}
can also be applied to

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\mu_{t}(\cdot+k,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot),

indeed the previous one is a periodic solution of the equation.

Theorem 1.4
.

Let
\mu_{0}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}\times[0,+\infty)\times[-1,1])
and
\mu_{t}
the mild solution of (
1.1.2
) with initial datum
\mu_{0}
. Then for any
\eta\in{\mathcal{S}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
it holds:

\displaystyle\left\|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}dx\eta(x)\mu_{t}(x,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)\right\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}\times[0,+\infty)\times[-1,1])}\xrightarrow[t\to+\infty]{}0,

where
{\mathcal{S}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is the Schwartz space of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

Let us point out that the theorems above do not require any assumption about the sign or the total mass of
\mu_{0}
, and also that the convergence result in Theorem
1.4
of course can not be improved by a convergence with respect to
L^{1}
norm because the total mass of
\mu_{t}
is preserved in time.

1.2.1.
Outline of the paper.

In Section
2
we recall and prove some properties of
\Pi
,
Q^{(n)}
,
E^{(n)}
,
f
and
g^{k}
defined in Subsection
1.3
. Then, in Section
3
we focus on the existence and the uniqueness of the mild solutions in
L^{p}
of the three equations, and we spend a few lines about the stationary solutions. In Section
4
we prove Theorem
1.1
and all the preliminary Lemmas we need for this purpose. In Section
5
we first focus on Theorem
1.2
, whose proof is quite similar to the proof of Theorem
1.1
. Then, we use it to prove Theorems
1.3
and
1.4
.

1.3.
Notations and Definitions.

1.3.1.
Notations.

As we said in the introduction, we will denote by
\mathbb{T}^{2}:=\mathbb{R}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}^{2}
the two-dimensional flat torus, and by
\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}:=\mathbb{R}/(2\pi\mathbb{Z})
the one-dimensional flat torus with period
2\pi
. This last notations may be uncommon but we decided to use it since using only
\mathbb{T}^{1}
could create misunderstandings about the period.

We also denote by
\langle f\rangle
the integral of
f
over the space it is defined on.

Moreover, for
\theta\in\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}
, we denote
v(\theta):=(\cos\theta,\sin\theta)
and
v^{\perp}(\theta):=(-\sin\theta,\cos\theta)
.

1.3.2.
Definitions.

Here we define some quantities that we will need in the following.
First, we recall from
[
6
,
15
,
4
]
that for
Q
and
E
as in Definitions
1.1
and
1.2
it holds:

\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\int_{-1}^{1}dhQ(s,h|h^{\prime})=1,\qquad\forall h^{\prime}\in[-1,1],\quad\textrm{ and }\quad\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\int_{-1}^{1}dhE(s,h)=1.

Other properties are stated in Section
2
. Recall also the definition of the transition probability
\Pi(h|h^{\prime})
in
[
7
]
, that is, the probability that the impact parameter of the next collision is
h
if in the previous one it was
h^{\prime}
.

Definition 1.4
.

The transition probability
\Pi:[-1,1]\times[-1,1]\to(0,+\infty)
is

\displaystyle\Pi(h|h^{\prime}):=\int_{0}^{\infty}dsQ(s,h|h^{\prime}).

By Definition
1.1
, we know that
\Pi
writes as

\displaystyle\Pi(h|h^{\prime}):=\frac{6}{\pi^{2}}\frac{\log(1+h)-\log(1+h^{\prime})}{h-h^{\prime}}\quad\forall|h^{\prime}|\leq h,

and that it has the symmetries

\displaystyle\Pi(h|h^{\prime})=\Pi(h^{\prime}|h)=\Pi(-h|-h^{\prime})>0\hskip 8.50012pt\forall(h,h^{\prime})\in[-1,1]^{2},\hskip 8.50012pt\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}\Pi(h|h^{\prime})=1\hskip 8.50012pt\forall h\in[-1,1].

We also need a generalization of the kernel
Q
, as follows.

Definition 1.5
.

The kernels
Q^{(n)}:[0,+\infty)\times[-1,1]\times[-1,1]\to[0,+\infty)
are defined inductively

\displaystyle Q^{(n)}(s,h|h^{\prime}):=\int_{0}^{s}ds^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime\prime}Q(s-s^{\prime},h|h^{\prime\prime})Q^{(n-1)}(s^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime}|h^{\prime}),\qquad n\geq 2,

with
Q^{(1)}:=Q
of Definition
1.1
.

For fixed
n
,
Q^{(n)}(s,h|h^{\prime})
is to be understood as the probability (density) of having impact parameter
h
exactly
n
collisions and time
s
after a collision with impact parameter
h^{\prime}
.

We also define the functions
E^{(n)}
, extending the Definition
1.2
of
E
, but replacing
Q
by
Q^{(n)}
.

Definition 1.6
.

The functions
E^{(n)}:[0,+\infty)\times[-1,1]\to[0,+\infty)
are

\displaystyle E^{(n)}(s,h):=\int_{s}^{\infty}ds^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}Q^{(n)}(s^{\prime},h|h^{\prime}),\qquad n\geq 1,

with kernels
Q^{(n)}
by Definition
1.5
.

We will also use the following functions
f
, mostly in Section
4
, and
\{g^{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{R}^{2},k\neq(0,0)}
, mostly in Section
5
. We start by defining the function
h^{\prime\prime}(\theta,h^{\prime})
.

Definition 1.7
.

The function
h^{\prime\prime}:\mathbb{R}\times[-1,1]\to[-1,1]
is

h^{\prime\prime}(\theta,h^{\prime}):=\sin\left(\frac{\theta+2\pi-2\arcsin(h^{\prime})}{2}\right)\mathbbm{1}_{[2\arcsin(h^{\prime})-3\pi,2\arcsin(h^{\prime})-\pi)}(\theta).

Then we use
h^{\prime\prime}
to define
f
.

Definition 1.8
.

The function
f:\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}\times[0,+\infty)\times[-1,1]\times[-1,1]\to[0,+\infty)
is

\displaystyle f(\theta,t,h|h^{\prime}):=\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{\partial h^{\prime\prime}(\theta+2\ell\pi,h^{\prime})}{\partial\theta}\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}Q(t-t^{\prime},h|h^{\prime\prime}(\theta+2\ell\pi,h^{\prime}))Q(t^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime}(\theta+2\ell\pi,h^{\prime})|h^{\prime}).

Notice that by integrating
f
over
\theta
one gets exactly
Q^{(2)}
, as in Definition
1.5
:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}}d\theta f(\theta,t,h|h^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int_{2\arcsin(h^{\prime})-3\pi}^{2\arcsin(h^{\prime})-\pi}d\theta\frac{\partial h^{\prime\prime}(\theta,h^{\prime})}{\partial\theta}\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}Q(t-t^{\prime},h|h^{\prime\prime}(\theta,h^{\prime}))Q(t^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime}(\theta,h^{\prime})|h^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime\prime}\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}Q(t-t^{\prime},h|h^{\prime\prime})Q(t^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime}|h^{\prime})\hskip 8.19447pt\textrm{ changing variables }h^{\prime\prime}=h^{\prime\prime}(\theta,h^{\prime})

(1.3.1)

\displaystyle=Q^{(2)}(t,h|h^{\prime}),\hskip 8.19447pt\textrm{ by Definition \ref{def:Qn}}.

Finally, we also use
h^{\prime\prime}
to define
g^{k}
.

Definition 1.9
.

For
k\in\mathbb{R}^{2},k\neq(0,0)
, and
h^{\prime\prime}
as in Definition
1.7
, the functions

g^{k}:\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}\times[0,+\infty)\times[-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}\times[-1,1]\to\mathbb{C},

are defined as

\displaystyle g^{k}(\theta,t,h|\theta^{\prime},h^{\prime}):=\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{\partial h^{\prime\prime}}{\partial\theta}e^{2\pi itk\cdot v(\theta)}\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}Q(t-t^{\prime},h|h^{\prime\prime})Q(t^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime}|h^{\prime})e^{2\pi it^{\prime}k\cdot[v(\theta^{\prime}-\pi+2\arcsin(h^{\prime}))-v(\theta)]},

with

h^{\prime\prime}:=h^{\prime\prime}(\theta-\theta^{\prime}+2\ell\pi,h^{\prime})\textrm{ from Definition \ref{defh''}}.

Notice also that
g^{(0,0)}
is not included in the previous Definition because one would have

g^{(0,0)}(\theta,t,h|\theta^{\prime},h^{\prime})=f(\theta-\theta^{\prime},t,h|h^{\prime}),

and also that for any
k
it holds
|g^{k}(\theta,t,h|\theta^{\prime},h^{\prime})|\leq f(\theta-\theta^{\prime},t,h|h^{\prime})
, with
f
from Definition
1.8
.

2.
Properties of the collision operator and related functions.

In this preliminary Section we study the properties of collision kernel
Q
and functions derived from it.

2.1.
Properties of
Q
and
Q^{(n)}
.

First we recall some basic properties of
Q
of Definition
1.1
.

2.1.1.
Behavior of
Q
for large
s
.

For fixed
h,h^{\prime}\in(-1,1)
,
Q
is compactly supported in
s
. But we need a bound not depending on
h,h^{\prime}
, and therefore we assert that there exists a constant
C>0
such that

(2.1.1)

\displaystyle Q(s,h|h^{\prime})\leq\frac{C}{s+1}\quad\forall s\in[0,+\infty),h,h^{\prime}\in[-1,1].

As pointed out in
[
7
,
15
]
, this can be easily derived from Definition
1.1
because, if
|h^{\prime}|\leq h
then

•

if
h-h^{\prime}\leq\frac{1}{2}
, then
\frac{1}{1+h^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{\underbrace{1+h}_{\geq 1}-\underbrace{(h-h^{\prime})}_{\leq\frac{1}{2}}}\leq 2
and therefore

Q(s,h|h^{\prime})=0\quad\forall s\geq 2,h>0,h^{\prime}\in[h-\frac{1}{2},h],

•

instead if
h-h^{\prime}\geq\frac{1}{2}
then, since
\frac{1}{1+h}\leq 1,\quad\forall s\geq 1
one has

Q(s,h|h^{\prime})=\frac{6}{\pi^{2}}\frac{\frac{1}{s}-(1+h^{\prime})}{h-h^{\prime}}\leq\frac{6}{\pi^{2}}\frac{2}{s}.

Since
Q
is also bounded, this proves (
2.1.1
) for
|h^{\prime}|\leq h
, and thanks to the symmetries of
Q
this exhausts all the other cases and proves (
2.1.1
).

Now we study the properties of
Q^{(n)}
, from Definition
1.5
, which we collect in the following Lemma.

Lemma 2.1
.

Q^{(n)}
has the following three properties:

(2.1.2)

\displaystyle Q^{(n)}(s,h|h^{\prime})

\displaystyle=Q^{(n)}(s,h^{\prime}|h);

(2.1.3)

\displaystyle Q^{(n)}(s,h|h^{\prime})

\displaystyle=Q^{(n)}(s,-h|-h^{\prime});

(2.1.4)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\int_{-1}^{1}dhQ^{(n)}(s,h|h^{\prime})

\displaystyle=1\textrm{ and in particular }E^{(n)}\leq 1.

Proof.

To begin with, we prove (
2.1.2
). For this purpose, it is sufficient to observe that, if
s_{0}:=s
and
s_{n}:=0
, since
Q
is symmetric we can rewrite
Q^{(n)}
as

\displaystyle Q^{(n)}(s,h_{0}|h_{n})

\displaystyle=\int_{s_{0}>s_{1}>\dots>s_{n-1}>0}\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}ds_{i}\int_{[-1,1]^{n-1}}\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}dh_{i}\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}Q(s_{i}-s_{i+1},h_{i}|h_{i+1})

\displaystyle=\int_{s_{0}>s_{1}>\dots>s_{n-1}>0}\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}ds_{i}\int_{[-1,1]^{n-1}}\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}dh_{i}\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}Q(s_{i}-s_{i+1},h_{i+1}|h_{i}),

and if we change variables
\tau_{i}:=s-s_{n-i},k_{i}=h_{n-i}
we get

\displaystyle Q^{(n)}(s,h_{0}|h_{n})

\displaystyle=\int_{\tau_{0}>\tau_{1}>\dots>\tau_{n-1}>0}\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}d\tau_{i}\int_{[-1,1]^{n-1}}\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}dk_{i}\underbrace{\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}Q(\tau_{n-1-i}-\tau_{n-i},k_{n-i-1}|k_{n-i})}_{=\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}Q(\tau_{i}-\tau_{i+1},k_{i}|k_{i+1})}

\displaystyle=Q^{(n)}(s,h_{n}|h_{0}).

Then, (
2.1.3
) can be proven inductively on
n
, indeed by changing variables
h^{\prime\prime}\mapsto-h^{\prime\prime}
in Definition
1.5
, we get

\displaystyle Q^{(n)}(s,-h|-h^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{s}ds^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime\prime}Q(s-s^{\prime},-h|h^{\prime\prime})Q^{(n-1)}(s^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime}|-h^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{s}ds^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime\prime}\underbrace{Q(s-s^{\prime},-h|-h^{\prime\prime})}_{=Q(s-s^{\prime},h|h^{\prime\prime})}\underbrace{Q^{(n-1)}(s^{\prime},-h^{\prime\prime}|-h^{\prime})}_{Q^{(n-1)}(s^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime}|h^{\prime})\textrm{ by inductive hypothesis}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{s}ds^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime\prime}Q(s-s^{\prime},h|h^{\prime\prime})Q^{(n-1)}(s^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime}|h^{\prime})

\displaystyle=Q^{(n)}(s,h|h^{\prime})\textrm{ by Definition }\ref{def:Qn}.

Lastly, to prove (
2.1.4
), we only use that
Q
preserves
L^{1}
norm, indeed

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\int_{-1}^{1}dhQ^{(n)}(s,h|h^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\int_{-1}^{1}dh\int_{0}^{s}ds^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime\prime}Q(s-s^{\prime},h|h^{\prime\prime})Q^{(n-1)}(s^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime}|h^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}ds^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime\prime}Q^{(n-1)}(s^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime}|h^{\prime})\underbrace{\int_{s^{\prime}}^{\infty}ds\int_{-1}^{1}dhQ(s-s^{\prime},h|h^{\prime\prime})}_{=1}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}ds^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime\prime}Q^{(n-1)}(s^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime}|h^{\prime})=1\textrm{ by inductive hypothesis}.

∎

2.2.
Properties of
E
and
E^{(n)}
.

To begin with, we recall some properties of
E
. We now focus on the
(s,h)
where
E
is supported on.

2.2.1.
Support of
E
.

The structure of the support of
E
is easily understood: for
h\geq 0
(
h<0
is symmetric because
E(s,-h)=E(s,h)
), we have

\displaystyle E(s,h)=0\textrm{ if and only if }Q(s^{\prime},h|h^{\prime})=0\quad\forall s^{\prime}\geq s,h^{\prime}\in[-1,1],

that is

\displaystyle E(s,h)=0\textrm{ if and only if }\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcr}s^{\prime}\geq\frac{1}{1+h^{\prime}}\hskip 8.19447pt\forall h^{\prime}\in[-h,h],s^{\prime}\geq s,\\
s^{\prime}\geq\frac{1}{1+h}\hskip 8.19447pt\forall s^{\prime}\geq s,h^{\prime}\in(h,1],\\
s^{\prime}\geq\frac{1}{1-h}\hskip 8.19447pt\forall h^{\prime}\in[-1,-h),s^{\prime}\geq s,\end{array}\right.\textrm{ that is if and only if }s\geq\frac{1}{1-h}.

Therefore

(2.2.1)

\displaystyle\textrm{Support of $E$ }=\left\{(s,h):h\in[-1,1],0\leq s\leq\frac{1}{1-|h|}\right\},

and in particular
E(s,h)>0\quad\forall h\in[-1,1],0\leq s<1
.

Figure 2.2.1.
The support of
E
is defined by the curve

\{s\geq 1,s=\frac{1}{1-|h|}\}\cup\{s=0,h\in[-1,1]\}\cup\{s\geq 0,h=\pm 1\}
.

Moreover we have the following asymptotic estimate.

Lemma 2.2

(
[
15
]
)
.

There exists a constant
C>0
such that

\displaystyle E(s,h)\leq\frac{C}{s+1}\mathbbm{1}_{s\leq\frac{1}{1-|h|}},\quad\forall s\in[0,+\infty),h\in[-1,1].

The previous Lemma can be obtained by direct computations on
Q
. The main consequence of this Lemma is that, as a function of
s
, the support of
E
is compact for any
h\in(-1,1)
and that, for fixed
s
,
E
is non zero only in an interval (in
h
) whose amplitude is
\frac{2}{s}
.

Moreover by
[
6
,
15
]
we have

(2.2.2)

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{1}dhE(s,h)\simeq\frac{1}{\pi^{2}s^{2}}\textrm{ and therefore }\int_{s}^{\infty}ds^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dhE(s^{\prime},h)\simeq\frac{1}{\pi^{2}s},

but the rougher estimate
\int_{s}^{\infty}ds^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dhE(s^{\prime},h)\leq\frac{C}{s+1}
can also be proved by using Lemma
2.2
and is sufficient for our purposes.
Then, in the following Lemma we collect some properties of the function
E^{(n)}
,
n\geq 1
.

Lemma 2.3
.

\forall n\in\mathbb{N}
,
E^{(n)}
of Definition
1.6
writes also as:

(2.2.3)

\displaystyle E^{(n)}(s,h)

\displaystyle=E^{(n-1)}(s,h)+\int_{0}^{s}ds^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}Q^{(n-1)}(s-s^{\prime},h|h^{\prime})E(s^{\prime},h^{\prime}),\quad n\geq 2.

\displaystyle E^{(n)}(s,h)

\displaystyle=E^{(n-1)}(s,h)+\int_{0}^{s}ds^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}Q(s-s^{\prime},h|h^{\prime})E^{(n-1)}(s^{\prime},h^{\prime})

(2.2.4)

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{s}ds^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}Q(s-s^{\prime},h|h^{\prime})E^{(n-2)}(s^{\prime},h^{\prime}),\quad n\geq 3.

Moreover
E^{(n)}
has the following properties:

(2.2.5)

\displaystyle E^{(n)}(s,h)=E^{(n)}(s,-h),

(2.2.6)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\int_{-1}^{1}dhE^{(n)}(s,h)=n,

(2.2.7)

\displaystyle E^{(n)}(s,h)\leq\frac{c_{n}}{s+1},\quad c_{n}>0,

(2.2.8)

\displaystyle\int_{s}^{\infty}ds^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dhE^{(n)}(s^{\prime},h)\leq\frac{c_{n}^{\prime}}{s+1}.

Proof.

To begin with, we prove property (
2.2.3
). We first look at the identity

\displaystyle E(s,h)

\displaystyle=\int_{s}^{\infty}ds^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}Q(s^{\prime},h|h^{\prime})

\displaystyle=1-\int_{0}^{s}ds^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}Q(s^{\prime},h|h^{\prime})

\displaystyle=1-\int_{0}^{s}ds^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}Q(s-s^{\prime},h|h^{\prime}).

Taking the convolution with
Q^{(n-1)}
of both sides, we get

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{s}ds^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}Q^{(n-1)}(s-s^{\prime},h|h^{\prime})E(s^{\prime},h^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{s}ds^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}Q^{(n-1)}(s-s^{\prime},h|h^{\prime})

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{s}ds^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}Q^{(n-1)}(s-s^{\prime},h|h^{\prime})\int_{0}^{s^{\prime}}ds^{\prime\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime\prime}Q(s^{\prime}-s^{\prime\prime},h^{\prime}|h^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=1-E^{(n-1)}(s,h)

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{s}ds^{\prime\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime\prime}\int_{0}^{s-s^{\prime\prime}}ds^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}Q^{(n-1)}(s-s^{\prime\prime}-s^{\prime},h|h^{\prime})Q(s^{\prime},h^{\prime}|h^{\prime\prime}).

The integral of the third summand in the right hand side of the above inequality can be also written as:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{s-s^{\prime\prime}}ds^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}Q^{(n-1)}(s-s^{\prime\prime}-s^{\prime},h|h^{\prime})Q(s^{\prime},h^{\prime}|h^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{s-s^{\prime\prime}}ds^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}\underbrace{Q^{(n-1)}(s^{\prime},h|h^{\prime})}_{=Q^{(n-1)}(s^{\prime},h^{\prime}|h)\textrm{ by }\eqref{Qnsimm}}\underbrace{Q(s-s^{\prime\prime}-s^{\prime},h^{\prime}|h^{\prime\prime})}_{=Q(s-s^{\prime\prime}-s^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime}|h^{\prime})\textrm{ by }\eqref{Qnsimm}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{s-s^{\prime\prime}}ds^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}Q(s-s^{\prime\prime}-s^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime}|h^{\prime})Q^{(n-1)}(s^{\prime},h^{\prime}|h)

\displaystyle=Q^{(n)}(s-s^{\prime\prime},h^{\prime\prime}|h)\textrm{ by Definition }\ref{def:Qn}

\displaystyle=Q^{(n)}(s-s^{\prime\prime},h|h^{\prime\prime})\textrm{ for the symmetry property }\eqref{Qnsimm},

hence recalling the previous expression we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{s}ds^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}Q^{(n-1)}(s-s^{\prime},h|h^{\prime})E(s^{\prime},h^{\prime})

\displaystyle=1-E^{(n-1)}(s,h)-\underbrace{\int_{0}^{s}ds^{\prime\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime\prime}Q^{(n)}(s^{\prime\prime},h|h^{\prime\prime})}_{=1-E^{(n)}(s,h)\textrm{ thanks to }\eqref{Qnint1}\textrm{ and Definition }\ref{def:En}}

\displaystyle=E^{(n)}(s,h)-E^{(n-1)}(s,h),

and this proves property (
2.2.3
).

Property (
2.2.4
) follows from (
2.2.3
): if
n\geq 3
we have:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{s}ds^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}Q^{(n-1)}(s-s^{\prime},h|h^{\prime})E(s^{\prime},h^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{s}ds^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}E(s^{\prime},h^{\prime})\underbrace{\int_{0}^{s-s^{\prime}}ds^{\prime\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime\prime}Q(s-s^{\prime}-s^{\prime\prime},h|h^{\prime\prime})Q^{(n-2)}(s^{\prime\prime},h^{\prime\prime}|h^{\prime})}_{\textrm{ by Definition \ref{def:Qn} of }Q^{(n-1)}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{s}ds^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}E(s^{\prime},h^{\prime})\int_{s^{\prime}}^{s}ds^{\prime\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime\prime}Q(s-s^{\prime\prime},h|h^{\prime\prime})Q^{(n-2)}(s^{\prime\prime}-s^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime}|h^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{s}ds^{\prime\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime\prime}Q(s-s^{\prime\prime},h|h^{\prime\prime})\underbrace{\int_{0}^{s^{\prime\prime}}ds^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}E(s^{\prime},h^{\prime})Q^{(n-2)}(s^{\prime\prime}-s^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime}|h^{\prime})}_{=E^{(n-1)}(s^{\prime\prime},h^{\prime\prime})-E^{(n-2)}(s^{\prime\prime},h^{\prime\prime})\textrm{ thanks to }\eqref{espr:En1}},

that is, by using again property (
2.2.3
), we have

\displaystyle E^{(n)}(s,h)-E^{(n-1)}(s,h)=\int_{0}^{s}ds^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}Q(s-s^{\prime},h|h^{\prime})\left[E^{(n-1)}(s^{\prime},h^{\prime})-E^{(n-2)}(s^{\prime},h^{\prime})\right],

that is (
2.2.4
).

The other three properties follow from the first and the second ones, and also from the fact that they hold for
n=1
. We begin with the proof of (
2.2.5
): by changing variables
h^{\prime}\mapsto-h^{\prime}
in Definition
1.6
of
E^{(n)}
, we have

\displaystyle E^{(n)}(s,-h)

\displaystyle=\int_{s}^{\infty}ds^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}Q^{(n)}(s^{\prime},-h|h^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int_{s}^{\infty}ds^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}\underbrace{Q^{(n)}(s^{\prime},-h|-h^{\prime})}_{=Q^{(n)}(s^{\prime},h|h^{\prime})\textrm{ thanks to }\eqref{Qnsegno}\textrm{ of Lemma \ref{proprQn}}}\textrm{ changing variables }h^{\prime}\to-h^{\prime}

\displaystyle=E^{(n)}(s,h).

To prove (
2.2.6
) we proceed by induction. It follows from (
2.2.3
), indeed

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\int_{-1}^{1}dhE^{(n)}(s,h)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\int_{-1}^{1}dhE^{(n-1)}(s,h)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\int_{-1}^{1}dh\int_{0}^{s}ds^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}Q^{(n-1)}(s-s^{\prime},h|h^{\prime})E(s^{\prime},h^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\int_{-1}^{1}dhE^{(n-1)}(s,h)}_{=n-1\textrm{ by inductive hypothesis}}

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{\infty}ds^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}E(s^{\prime},h^{\prime})\underbrace{\int_{s^{\prime}}^{\infty}ds\int_{-1}^{1}dhQ^{(n-1)}(s-s^{\prime},h|h^{\prime})}_{=1\textrm{ by }\eqref{Qnint1}}

\displaystyle=n-1+1=n.

To prove (
2.2.7
) we proceed by induction too. By using again (
2.2.4
) we infer that

\displaystyle E^{(n)}(s,h)

\displaystyle\leq E^{(n-1)}(s,h)+\int_{0}^{s}ds^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}Q(s-s^{\prime},h|h^{\prime})E^{(n-1)}(s^{\prime},h^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\underbrace{E^{(n-1)}(s,h)}_{\leq\frac{c_{n-1}}{s+1}}+\int_{0}^{s/2}ds^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}\underbrace{Q(s-s^{\prime},h|h^{\prime})}_{\leq\frac{C}{s-s^{\prime}+1}\leq\frac{2C}{s+2}\textrm{ by \eqref{decQ}}}E^{(n-1)}(s^{\prime},h^{\prime})

\displaystyle+\int_{s/2}^{s}ds^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}Q(s-s^{\prime},h|h^{\prime})\underbrace{E^{(n-1)}(s^{\prime},h^{\prime})}_{\leq\frac{c_{n-1}}{s^{\prime}+1}\leq\frac{2c_{n-1}}{s+2}\textrm{ by inductive hypothesis }}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c_{n-1}}{s+1}+\frac{2C}{s+1}\underbrace{\int_{0}^{s/2}ds^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}E^{(n-1)}(s^{\prime},h^{\prime})}_{\leq n-1\textrm{ by }\eqref{intEn}}

\displaystyle+\frac{2c_{n-1}}{s+1}\underbrace{\int_{s/2}^{s}ds^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}Q(s-s^{\prime},h|h^{\prime})}_{\leq 1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3c_{n-1}+2C(n-1)}{s+1}=:\frac{c_{n}}{s+1}.

Lastly, we are proving also (
2.2.8
) by induction. Thanks to (
2.2.3
), we have

\displaystyle\int_{s}^{\infty}ds^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dhE^{(n)}(s^{\prime},h)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\int_{s}^{\infty}ds^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dhE^{(n-1)}(s^{\prime},h)}_{\leq\frac{c_{n-1}^{\prime}}{s}\textrm{ by inductive hypothesis}}

\displaystyle+\int_{s}^{\infty}ds^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh\int_{0}^{s^{\prime}}ds^{\prime\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}Q^{(n-1)}(s^{\prime}-s^{\prime\prime},h|h^{\prime})E(s^{\prime\prime},h^{\prime}).

Now since we are applying the inductive hypothesis to the first of the previous two summands, we look at the second one, which we rewrite as

\displaystyle\int_{s}^{\infty}ds^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh\int_{0}^{s^{\prime}}ds^{\prime\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}Q^{(n-1)}(s^{\prime}-s^{\prime\prime},h|h^{\prime})E(s^{\prime\prime},h^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}ds^{\prime\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}E(s^{\prime\prime},h^{\prime})\int_{\max\{s^{\prime\prime},s\}}^{\infty}ds^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dhQ^{(n-1)}(s^{\prime}-s^{\prime\prime},h|h^{\prime}),

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{s}ds^{\prime\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}E(s^{\prime\prime},h^{\prime})\underbrace{\int_{s}^{\infty}ds^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dhQ^{(n-1)}(s^{\prime}-s^{\prime\prime},h|h^{\prime})}_{=E^{(n-1)}(s-s^{\prime\prime},h^{\prime})}

\displaystyle+\int_{s}^{\infty}ds^{\prime\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}E(s^{\prime\prime},h^{\prime})\underbrace{\int_{s^{\prime\prime}}^{\infty}ds^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dhQ^{(n-1)}(s^{\prime}-s^{\prime\prime},h|h^{\prime})}_{=1\textrm{ by }\eqref{Qnint1}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{s}ds^{\prime\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}E(s^{\prime\prime},h^{\prime})E^{(n-1)}(s-s^{\prime\prime},h^{\prime})+\int_{s}^{\infty}ds^{\prime\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}E(s^{\prime\prime},h^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{s/2}ds^{\prime\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}\underbrace{E(s^{\prime\prime},h^{\prime})}_{\leq 1}E^{(n-1)}(s-s^{\prime\prime},h^{\prime})

\displaystyle+\int_{s/2}^{s}ds^{\prime\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}E(s^{\prime\prime},h^{\prime})\underbrace{E^{(n-1)}(s-s^{\prime\prime},h^{\prime})}_{\leq 1}+\int_{s}^{\infty}ds^{\prime\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}E(s^{\prime\prime},h^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\int_{s/2}^{\infty}ds^{\prime\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}E^{(n-1)}(s^{\prime\prime},h^{\prime})+\int_{s/2}^{\infty}ds^{\prime\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}E(s^{\prime\prime},h^{\prime})

\displaystyle+\int_{s}^{\infty}ds^{\prime\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}E(s^{\prime\prime},h^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2c_{n-1}^{\prime}+2C}{s+2}+\frac{C}{s+1}\textrm{ by inductive hypothesis and with }C\textrm{ derived by }\eqref{decintE}.

This concludes the proof of Lemma
2.3
.
∎

2.3.
Properties of
\Pi
,
f
and
g^{k}
.

In this Subsection we want to prove some properties of the functions written above.

2.3.1.
Properties of
\Pi
.

\Pi
is not finite for any choice of
h
and
h^{\prime}
, indeed
\Pi(1|-1)=+\infty
, but we can prove that
\Pi
diverges at most logarithmically at most.

Lemma 2.4
.

For any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, the transition kernel
\Pi
in Definition
1.4
satisfies

\Pi(h|h^{\prime})\leq\frac{6}{\pi^{2}}\max\left\{\frac{1}{\varepsilon},\frac{\log 2-\log(1-|h|)}{2(1-\varepsilon)}\right\}.

Proof.

We prove this property case by case, and such cases are not necessarily disjoints.

We begin with the following case:

(2.3.1)

\displaystyle\textrm{If }h\textrm{ and }h^{\prime}\textrm{ have the same sign, then }\Pi(h|h^{\prime})\leq\frac{6}{\pi^{2}}.

To prove inequality (
2.3.1
) we can notice that, since the hypothesis is symmetric in
h
and
h^{\prime}
, we can restrict to the case
h\geq h^{\prime}\geq 0
, where we have

\displaystyle\hskip 8.19447pt\Pi(h|h^{\prime})=\frac{6}{\pi^{2}}\frac{\log(1+h)-\log(1+h^{\prime})}{h-h^{\prime}}=\frac{6}{\pi^{2}}\frac{1}{1+\xi},\hskip 8.19447pt\xi\in[h^{\prime},h]\subseteq[0,1],\textrm{ and therefore }\Pi(h|h^{\prime})\leq\frac{6}{\pi^{2}}.

Now let us fix
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
.

(2.3.2)

\displaystyle\textrm{If }|h|,|h^{\prime}|\leq 1-\varepsilon\textrm{ then }\Pi(h|h^{\prime})\leq\frac{6}{\varepsilon\pi^{2}}.

This case is similar to the previous one, and since the hypothesis in (
2.3.2
) is symmetric with respect to exchanging
h
and
h^{\prime}
, we can restrict to the case
h\geq|h^{\prime}|
, indeed by symmetry this also exhausts the other possibilities:

\displaystyle\Pi(h|h^{\prime})=\frac{6}{\pi^{2}}\frac{\log(1+h)-\log(1+h^{\prime})}{h-h^{\prime}}=\frac{6}{\pi^{2}}\frac{1}{1+\xi},\hskip 8.50012pt\xi\in[h^{\prime},h]\subseteq[-h,h]\subseteq[-1+\varepsilon,1-\varepsilon],

therefore

\Pi(h|h^{\prime})\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\frac{6}{\pi^{2}}.

Therefore in the previous cases
\Pi
is bounded by a constant that depends on
\varepsilon
.
The same holds in the following case:

(2.3.3)

\displaystyle\textrm{If }|h|\leq 1-\varepsilon\leq|h^{\prime}|\textrm{ or }|h^{\prime}|\leq 1-\varepsilon\leq|h|,\textrm{ and }hh^{\prime}\leq 0,\textrm{ then }\Pi(h|h^{\prime})\leq\frac{6}{\varepsilon\pi^{2}}.

To prove property (
2.3.3
) we look at the case
|h^{\prime}|\leq 1-\varepsilon\leq|h|
:

\displaystyle\textrm{if }h>0\geq h^{\prime},

\displaystyle\textrm{ then }h\geq 1-\varepsilon\geq|h^{\prime}|=-h^{\prime},\textrm{ then }\Pi(h|h^{\prime})=\frac{6}{\pi^{2}}\frac{\log(1+h)-\log(1+h^{\prime})}{h-h^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\frac{6}{\pi^{2}(1+\xi)},\textrm{ with }\xi\in[h^{\prime},h]\subseteq[-1+\varepsilon,1],\textrm{ therefore }\Pi(h|h^{\prime})\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\frac{6}{\pi^{2}},

\displaystyle\textrm{if }h<0\leq h^{\prime},

\displaystyle\textrm{ then }-h=|h|\geq 1-\varepsilon\geq|h^{\prime}|=|-h^{\prime}|=h^{\prime}\geq-h^{\prime},\textrm{ then }\Pi(h|h^{\prime})=\Pi(-h|-h^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\frac{6}{\pi^{2}}\frac{\log(1-h)-\log(1-h^{\prime})}{(-h)-(-h^{\prime})}=\frac{1}{1+\xi},\textrm{ with }\xi\in[-h^{\prime},-h]\subseteq[-1+\varepsilon,1],

\displaystyle\textrm{ therefore }\Pi(h|h^{\prime})\leq\frac{6}{\varepsilon\pi^{2}}.

By symmetry this exhausts also the case
|h|\leq 1-\varepsilon\leq|h^{\prime}|
and therefore (
2.3.3
) is proved.

It remains to be proven that

(2.3.4)

\displaystyle\textrm{If }|h|,|h^{\prime}|\geq 1-\varepsilon\textrm{ and }hh^{\prime}<0,\textrm{ then }\Pi(h|h^{\prime})\leq\frac{6(\log 2-\log(1-|h|))}{2\pi^{2}(1-\varepsilon)}.

We prove (
2.3.4
) case by case, as follows:

\displaystyle\textrm{if }h\geq-h^{\prime}\geq 1-\varepsilon\hskip 8.19447pt\textrm{ then }\hskip 8.19447pt\Pi(h|h^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\frac{6}{\pi^{2}}\frac{\log(1+h)-\log(1+h^{\prime})}{\underbrace{h-h^{\prime}}_{\geq 2(1-\varepsilon)}}\leq\frac{6(\log 2-\log(1+h^{\prime}))}{2\pi^{2}(1-\varepsilon)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{6(\log 2-\log(1-|h|))}{2\pi^{2}(1-\varepsilon)},

\displaystyle\textrm{if }-h^{\prime}\geq h\geq 1-\varepsilon\hskip 8.19447pt\textrm{ then }\hskip 8.19447pt\Pi(h|h^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\Pi(-h|-h^{\prime})=\Pi(-h^{\prime}|-h)=\frac{6}{\pi^{2}}\frac{\log(1-h^{\prime})-\log(1-h)}{\underbrace{(-h^{\prime})-(-h)}_{\geq 2(1-\varepsilon)}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{6(\log 2-\log(1-h))}{2\pi^{2}(1-\varepsilon)}=\frac{6(\log 2-\log(1-|h|))}{2\pi^{2}(1-\varepsilon)},

\displaystyle\textrm{if }h^{\prime}\geq-h\geq 1-\varepsilon\hskip 8.19447pt\textrm{ then }\hskip 8.19447pt\Pi(h|h^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\Pi(h^{\prime}|h)=\frac{6}{\pi^{2}}\frac{\log(1+h^{\prime})-\log(1+h)}{\underbrace{h^{\prime}-h}_{\geq 2(1-\varepsilon)}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{6(\log 2-\log(1+h))}{2\pi^{2}(1-\varepsilon)}=\frac{6(\log 2-\log(1-|h|))}{2\pi^{2}(1-\varepsilon)},

\displaystyle\textrm{if }-h\geq h^{\prime}\geq 1-\varepsilon\hskip 8.19447pt\textrm{ then }\hskip 8.19447pt\Pi(h|h^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\Pi(-h|-h^{\prime})=\frac{6}{\pi^{2}}\frac{\log(1-h)-\log(1-h^{\prime})}{\underbrace{(-h)-(-h^{\prime})}_{\geq 2(1-\varepsilon)}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{6(\log 2-\log(1-h^{\prime}))}{2\pi^{2}(1-\varepsilon)}\leq\frac{6(\log 2-\log(1+h))}{2\pi^{2}(1-\varepsilon)}

\displaystyle=\frac{6(\log 2-\log(1-|h|))}{2\pi^{2}(1-\varepsilon)}.

By collecting the estimates (
2.3.1
), (
2.3.2
), (
2.3.3
) and (
2.3.4
), since
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
implies
\frac{1}{\varepsilon}>1
, we get the desired conclusion.
∎

2.3.2.
Properties of
f
.

We study here the properties of the function
f
in Definition
1.8
.

Lemma 2.5
.

The function
f:\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}\times[0,+\infty)\times[-1,1]\times[-1,1]\to[0,+\infty)
in Definition
1.8
has the following properties:

(2.3.5)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}}d\theta\int_{0}^{\infty}dt\int_{-1}^{1}dhf(\theta,t,h|h^{\prime})=1\quad\forall h^{\prime}\in[-1,1],

(2.3.6)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}}d\theta\int_{0}^{\infty}dt\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}f(\theta,t,h|h^{\prime})=1\quad\forall h\in[-1,1],

(2.3.7)

\displaystyle f(\theta,t,h|h^{\prime})\leq\frac{C}{t+1}\qquad\forall(\theta,t,h|h^{\prime})\in\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}\times[0,+\infty)\times[-1,1]\times[-1,1],

(2.3.8)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime\prime}Q(t-t^{\prime},h|h^{\prime\prime})f(\theta+\pi-2\arcsin(h^{\prime\prime}),t^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime}|h^{\prime})\leq\frac{C}{t+1}.

Proof.

We begin with the proof of (
2.3.5
). We have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}}d\theta\int_{0}^{\infty}dt\int_{-1}^{1}dhf(\theta,t,h|h^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}dt\int_{-1}^{1}dh\int_{\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}}d\theta f(\theta,t,h|h^{\prime})\textrm{ changing integration order}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}dt\int_{-1}^{1}dhQ^{(2)}(t,h|h^{\prime})\textrm{ by property \eqref{intfQ2}}

\displaystyle=1\textrm{ by property \eqref{Qnint1} of Lemma \ref{proprQn}.}

The proof of (
2.3.6
) works in the same way:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}}d\theta\int_{0}^{\infty}dt\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}f(\theta,s,h|h^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}dt\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}}d\theta f(\theta,t,h|h^{\prime})\textrm{ changing integration order}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}dt\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}Q^{(2)}(t,h|h^{\prime})\textrm{ by property \eqref{intfQ2}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}dt\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}Q^{(2)}(t,h^{\prime}|h)=1\textrm{ by property \eqref{Qnsimm} of Lemma \ref{proprQn}}

\displaystyle=1\textrm{ by property \eqref{Qnint1} of Lemma \ref{proprQn}.}

As for (
2.3.7
), first recall
f
is obtained as

\displaystyle f(\theta,t,h|h^{\prime})=\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{\partial h^{\prime\prime}(\theta+2\ell\pi,h^{\prime})}{\partial\theta}\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}Q(t-t^{\prime},h|h^{\prime\prime}(\theta+2\ell\pi,h^{\prime}))Q(t^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime}(\theta+2\ell\pi,h^{\prime})|h^{\prime}),

with

\displaystyle h^{\prime\prime}(\theta,h^{\prime})=\sin\left(\frac{\theta+2\pi-2\arcsin(h^{\prime})}{2}\right)\mathbbm{1}_{[2\arcsin(h^{\prime})-3\pi,2\arcsin(h^{\prime})-\pi]}(\theta).

Since
\cos^{2}+\sin^{2}=1
and
f
is obtained by extending periodically its definition for

\theta\in[2\arcsin(h^{\prime})-3\pi,2\arcsin(h^{\prime})-\pi],

where
\cos\left(\frac{\theta+2\pi-2\arcsin(h^{\prime})}{2}\right)\geq 0
, we have

\displaystyle f(\theta,t,h|h^{\prime})=\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{\sqrt{1-h^{\prime\prime}(\theta+2\ell\pi,h^{\prime})^{2}}}{2}\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}Q(t-t^{\prime},h|h^{\prime\prime}(\theta+2\ell\pi,h^{\prime}))Q(t^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime}(\theta+2\ell\pi,h^{\prime})|h^{\prime}).

It is then sufficient to prove that there exists a constant
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{1-h^{\prime\prime 2}}}{2}\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}Q(t-t^{\prime},h|h^{\prime\prime})Q(t^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime}|h^{\prime})\leq\frac{C}{t+1}\quad\forall h,h^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime}\in[-1,1].

This holds because

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{1-h^{\prime\prime 2}}}{2}\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}Q(t-t^{\prime},h|h^{\prime\prime})Q(t^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime}|h^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{1-h^{\prime\prime 2}}}{2}\int_{0}^{\frac{t}{2}}dt^{\prime}\underbrace{Q(t-t^{\prime},h^{\prime}|h^{\prime\prime})}_{\leq\frac{C}{t-t^{\prime}+1}\leq\frac{2C}{t+2}\textrm{ by }\eqref{decQ}}Q(t^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime}|h)

\displaystyle+\frac{\sqrt{1-h^{\prime\prime 2}}}{2}\int_{\frac{t}{2}}^{t}dt^{\prime}Q(t-t^{\prime},h|h^{\prime\prime})\underbrace{Q(t^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime}|h)}_{\leq\frac{C}{t^{\prime}+1}\leq\frac{2C}{t+2}\textrm{ by }\eqref{decQ}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C\sqrt{1-h^{\prime\prime 2}}}{t+2}\left[\int_{0}^{\frac{t}{2}}dt^{\prime}Q(t^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime}|h)+\int_{\frac{t}{2}}^{t}dt^{\prime}Q(t-t^{\prime},h^{\prime}|h^{\prime\prime})\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C\sqrt{1-h^{\prime\prime 2}}}{t+2}\left[\Pi(h^{\prime\prime}|h)+\Pi(h^{\prime}|h^{\prime\prime})\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C\sqrt{1-h^{\prime\prime 2}}}{t+2}\frac{6}{\pi^{2}}\max\left\{\frac{1}{\varepsilon},\frac{\log 2-\log(1-|h^{\prime\prime}|)}{2(1-\varepsilon)}\right\},

where in the last inequality we used Lemma
2.4
.

Therefore

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{1-h^{\prime\prime 2}}}{2}\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}Q(t-t,h|h^{\prime\prime})Q(t^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime}|h^{\prime})\leq\frac{C\sqrt{1+|h^{\prime\prime}|}\sqrt{1-|h^{\prime\prime}|}}{t+2}\frac{6}{\pi^{2}}\max\left\{\frac{1}{\varepsilon},\frac{\log 2-\log(1-|h^{\prime\prime}|)}{2(1-\varepsilon)}\right\},

hence the desired conclusion, since the function
\sqrt{x}\log x
is bounded around
0
.

Lastly, (
2.3.8
) follows from property (
2.3.7
), indeed

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime\prime}Q(t-t^{\prime},h|h^{\prime\prime})f(\theta+\pi-2\arcsin(h^{\prime\prime}),t^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime}|h^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\frac{t}{2}}dt^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime\prime}Q(t-t^{\prime},h|h^{\prime\prime})\underbrace{f(\theta+\pi-2\arcsin(h^{\prime\prime}),t^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime}|h^{\prime})}_{\leq C\textrm{ by }\eqref{deceffe}}

\displaystyle+\int_{\frac{t}{2}}^{t}dt^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime\prime}Q(t-t^{\prime},h|h^{\prime\prime})\underbrace{f(\theta+\pi-2\arcsin(h^{\prime\prime}),t^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime}|h^{\prime})}_{\leq\frac{C}{t^{\prime}+1}\leq\frac{2C}{t+2}\textrm{ by }\eqref{deceffe}}

\displaystyle\leq C\underbrace{\int_{\frac{t}{2}}^{\infty}dt^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime\prime}Q(t^{\prime},h|h^{\prime\prime})}_{=E(\frac{t}{2},h)\leq\frac{C}{\frac{t}{2}+1}\textrm{ by Lemma \ref{decE}}}+\frac{2C}{s+2}\underbrace{\int_{0}^{\frac{t}{2}}dt^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime\prime}Q(t^{\prime},h|h^{\prime\prime})}_{\leq 1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C^{\prime}}{s+2}.

∎

2.3.3.
Properties of
g^{k}
.

We state here some properties of the function
g^{k}
introduced in Definition
1.9
.

Lemma 2.6
.

For
k\in\mathbb{R}^{2},k\neq(0,0)
, the function
g^{k}:\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}\times[0,+\infty)\times[-1,1]\times\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}\times[-1,1]\to\mathbb{C}
by Definition
1.9
has the following properties: for any
(\theta,t,h|\theta^{\prime},h^{\prime})

(2.3.9)

\displaystyle|g^{k}(\theta,t,h|\theta^{\prime},h^{\prime})|\leq\frac{C}{t+1},

(2.3.10)

\displaystyle\left|\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime\prime}Q(t-t^{\prime},h|h^{\prime\prime})e^{2\pi i(t-t^{\prime})k\cdot v(\theta)}g^{k}(\theta+\pi-2\arcsin(h^{\prime\prime}),t^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime}|\theta^{\prime},h^{\prime})\right|\leq\frac{C}{t+1},

(2.3.11)

\displaystyle\|g^{k}(\theta,\cdot,h|\cdot,\cdot)\|_{L^{1}}\leq 1-C^{\prime}\min\{1,|k|^{2}\}\hskip 17.00024pt\forall(\theta,h)\in\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}\times[-1,1],

where
C>0
and
C^{\prime}\in(0,1)
do not depend on
k\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
\theta\in\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}
or
h\in[-1,1]
.

Proof.

The properties (
2.3.9
) and (
2.3.10
) follow immediately from Definitions
1.9
and
1.8
of
g^{k}
and
f
, indeed by definition we have

|g^{k}(\theta,t,h|\theta^{\prime},h^{\prime})|\leq f(\theta-\theta^{\prime},s,h|h^{\prime}),

and since the estimates (
2.3.7
) and (
2.3.8
) apply to
f
the proof of the first two statements is concluded.

To prove the other two properties, we write
\|g^{k}(\theta,\cdot,h|\cdot,\cdot)\|_{L^{1}}
as

\displaystyle\|g^{k}(\theta,\cdot,h|\cdot,\cdot)\|_{L^{1}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}dt\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime\prime}\left|\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}Q(t-t^{\prime},h|h^{\prime\prime})Q(t^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime}|h^{\prime})e^{2\pi it^{\prime}k\cdot(v(\theta+\pi-2\arcsin(h^{\prime\prime}))-v(\theta))}\right|.

Now we prove (
2.3.11
). For this purpose we use the properties of
Q^{(2)}
introduced by Definition
1.5
.

Thanks to (
2.1.4
) of Lemma
2.1
,
Q^{(2)}
has integral 1. Hence we have

\displaystyle 1-\|g^{k}(\theta,\cdot,h|\cdot,\cdot)\|_{L^{1}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}dt\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime\prime}\left[\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}Q(t-t^{\prime},h|h^{\prime\prime})Q(t^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime}|h^{\prime})\right.

\displaystyle-\left.\left|\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}Q(t-t^{\prime},h|h^{\prime\prime})Q(t^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime}|h^{\prime})e^{2\pi it^{\prime}k\cdot(v(\theta+\pi-2\arcsin(h^{\prime\prime}))-v(\theta))}\right|\right]

\displaystyle\geq\int_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}dt\underbrace{\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}}_{=2}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime\prime}\left[\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}\underbrace{Q(t-t^{\prime},h|h^{\prime\prime})}_{=\frac{6}{\pi^{2}}}\underbrace{Q(t^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime}|h^{\prime})}_{=\frac{6}{\pi^{2}}}\right.

\displaystyle-\left.\left|\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}\underbrace{Q(t-t^{\prime},h|h^{\prime\prime})}_{=\frac{6}{\pi^{2}}}\underbrace{Q(t^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime}|h^{\prime})}_{=\frac{6}{\pi^{2}}}e^{2\pi it^{\prime}k\cdot(v(\theta+\pi-2\arcsin(h^{\prime\prime}))-v(\theta))}\right|\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{72}{\pi^{4}}\int_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}dt\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime\prime}\left[t-\left|\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}e^{it^{\prime}2\pi k\cdot(v(\theta+\pi-2\arcsin(h^{\prime\prime}))-v(\theta))}\right|\right],

and therefore, since

\left|\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}e^{it^{\prime}\omega}\right|=\left|\frac{e^{it\omega}-1}{\omega}\right|=t\frac{\sqrt{2(1-\cos(|\omega|t))}}{|\omega|t},

if
x(\theta,t,h^{\prime\prime}):=2\pi|k\cdot(v(\theta+\pi-2\arcsin(h^{\prime\prime}))-v(\theta))|t
, we got

(2.3.12)

\displaystyle 1-\|g^{k}(\theta,\cdot,h|\cdot,\cdot)\|_{L^{1}}\geq\frac{72}{\pi^{4}}\int_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}dtt\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime\prime}\left[1-\frac{\sqrt{2(1-\cos x(\theta,t,h^{\prime\prime}))}}{x(\theta,t,h^{\prime\prime})}\right].

First we want to estimate from below the integrand. To this purpose, notice that since by direct computations one can find a constant
c^{\prime\prime}>0
such that

1-\frac{\sqrt{2(1-\cos x)}}{x}\geq c^{\prime\prime}\min\{x,c^{\prime\prime}\}^{2},

by (
2.3.12
) one gets

(2.3.13)

\displaystyle 1-\|g^{k}(\theta,\cdot,h|\cdot,\cdot)\|_{L^{1}}\geq\frac{72c^{\prime\prime}}{\pi^{4}}\int_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}dtt\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime\prime}\min\left\{x(\theta,t,h^{\prime\prime}),c^{\prime\prime}\right\}^{2},

and therefore we finally have to bound from below
x(\theta,t,h^{\prime\prime})
, at least in a suitable region that we will denote by
A^{k}(\theta)
. To this purpose, we first observe that

\displaystyle x(\theta,t,h^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=2\pi|k\cdot(v(\theta+\pi-2\arcsin(h^{\prime\prime}))-v(\theta))|t

\displaystyle=4\pi t\sqrt{1-h^{\prime\prime 2}}|k|\left|\sqrt{1-h^{\prime\prime 2}}\hat{k}\cdot v(\theta)-h^{\prime\prime}\hat{k}\cdot v^{\perp}(\theta)\right|,

with
\hat{k}:=\frac{k}{|k|}
and
v^{\perp}(\theta)=(-\sin\theta,\cos\theta)
.

Now

\textrm{ if }k\cdot v^{\perp}(\theta)=0,\textrm{ then }\hat{k}\cdot v(\theta)=\pm 1\textrm{ and therefore }\left|\sqrt{1-h^{\prime\prime 2}}\hat{k}\cdot v(\theta)-h^{\prime\prime}\hat{k}\cdot v^{\perp}(\theta)\right|=\sqrt{1-h^{\prime\prime 2}},

thus we fix
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{3})
and in this case we define

(2.3.14)

\displaystyle A^{k}(\theta):=[-1+\delta,1-\delta],\textrm{ with measure }|A^{k}(\theta)|=2-2\delta,

such that for any
h^{\prime\prime}\in A^{k}(\theta)

(2.3.15)

\displaystyle x(\theta,t,h^{\prime\prime})=4\pi t\sqrt{1-h^{\prime\prime 2}}|k|\left|\sqrt{1-h^{\prime\prime 2}}\hat{k}\cdot v(\theta)-h^{\prime\prime}\hat{k}\cdot v^{\perp}(\theta)\right|=4\pi t(1-h^{\prime\prime 2})|k|\geq 4\pi t\delta|k|.

Therefore hereafter we can assume
\hat{k}\cdot v^{\perp}(\theta)\neq 0
. In this case

\left|\sqrt{1-h^{\prime\prime 2}}\hat{k}\cdot v(\theta)-h^{\prime\prime}\hat{k}\cdot v^{\perp}(\theta)\right|=0\Leftrightarrow h^{\prime\prime}=\hat{k}\cdot v(\theta)\textrm{sign}(\hat{k}\cdot v^{\perp}(\theta))=:h^{\prime\prime}(\theta),

and we can rearrange the previous term as follows:

\displaystyle\left|\sqrt{1-h^{\prime\prime 2}}\hat{k}\cdot v(\theta)-h^{\prime\prime}\hat{k}\cdot v^{\perp}(\theta)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|(\sqrt{1-h^{\prime\prime 2}}-\sqrt{1-h^{\prime\prime 2}(\theta)})\hat{k}\cdot v(\theta)-(h^{\prime\prime}-h^{\prime\prime}(\theta))\hat{k}\cdot v^{\perp}(\theta)\right|

\displaystyle=|h^{\prime\prime}-h^{\prime\prime}(\theta)|\left|\frac{h^{\prime\prime}(\theta)+h^{\prime\prime}}{\sqrt{1-h^{\prime\prime 2}}+\sqrt{1-h^{\prime\prime 2}(\theta)}}\hat{k}\cdot v(\theta)+\hat{k}\cdot v^{\perp}(\theta)\right|

\displaystyle=|h^{\prime\prime}-h^{\prime\prime}(\theta)|\left|\frac{(\hat{k}\cdot v(\theta))^{2}\textrm{sign}(\hat{k}\cdot v^{\perp}(\theta))+h^{\prime\prime}\hat{k}\cdot v(\theta)}{\sqrt{1-h^{\prime\prime 2}}+|\hat{k}\cdot v^{\perp}(\theta)|}+\hat{k}\cdot v^{\perp}(\theta)\right|

\displaystyle=|h^{\prime\prime}-h^{\prime\prime}(\theta)|\left|\frac{\textrm{sign}(\hat{k}\cdot v^{\perp}(\theta))+h^{\prime\prime}\hat{k}\cdot v(\theta)+\sqrt{1-h^{\prime\prime 2}}\hat{k}\cdot v^{\perp}(\theta)}{\sqrt{1-h^{\prime\prime 2}}+|\hat{k}\cdot v^{\perp}(\theta)|}\right|

(2.3.16)

\displaystyle=|h^{\prime\prime}-h^{\prime\prime}(\theta)|\frac{1+h^{\prime\prime}h^{\prime\prime}(\theta)+\sqrt{1-h^{\prime\prime 2}}\sqrt{1-h^{\prime\prime 2}(\theta)}}{\sqrt{1-h^{\prime\prime 2}}+|\hat{k}\cdot v^{\perp}(\theta)|}.

Now, for
\delta<\frac{1}{3}
fixed as before, we define the region

(2.3.17)

\displaystyle A^{k}(\theta):=\left\{h^{\prime\prime}\in[-1,1]:h^{\prime\prime}h^{\prime\prime}(\theta)\geq 0,|h^{\prime\prime}-h^{\prime\prime}(\theta)|\geq\delta,1-|h^{\prime\prime}|\geq\delta\right\},

with measure

|A^{k}(\theta)|\geq 1-3\delta.

Using the previous computation (
2.3.16
), for any
h^{\prime\prime}\in A^{k}(\theta)
we have

(2.3.18)

\displaystyle x(\theta,t,h^{\prime\prime})=4\pi t\underbrace{\sqrt{1-h^{\prime\prime 2}}}_{\geq\sqrt{\delta}}|k|\underbrace{|h^{\prime\prime}-h^{\prime\prime}(\theta)|}_{\geq\delta}\frac{1+\overbrace{h^{\prime\prime}h^{\prime\prime}(\theta)}^{\geq 0}+\overbrace{\sqrt{1-h^{\prime\prime 2}}\sqrt{1-h^{\prime\prime 2}(\theta)}}^{\geq 0}}{\underbrace{\sqrt{1-h^{\prime\prime 2}}}_{\leq 1}+\underbrace{|\hat{k}\cdot v^{\perp}(\theta)|}_{\leq 1}}\geq 2\pi t|k|\delta\sqrt{\delta},

and combining this estimate with (
2.3.13
) we have

\displaystyle 1-\|g^{k}(\theta,\cdot,h|\cdot,\cdot)\|_{L^{1}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{72c^{\prime\prime}}{\pi^{4}}\int_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}dtt\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime\prime}\min\left\{x(\theta,t,h^{\prime\prime}),c^{\prime\prime}\right\}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{72c^{\prime\prime}}{\pi^{4}}\int_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}dtt\int_{A^{k}(\theta)}dh^{\prime\prime}\min\left\{x(\theta,t,h^{\prime\prime}),c^{\prime\prime}\right\}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{72c^{\prime\prime}}{\pi^{4}}\int_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}dtt\int_{A^{k}(\theta)}dh^{\prime\prime}\min\left\{2\pi|k|\delta\sqrt{\delta}t,c^{\prime\prime}\right\}^{2}\textrm{ by \eqref{kvperp=0} and \eqref{stimabassox}}

\displaystyle=\frac{72c^{\prime\prime}}{\pi^{4}}\int_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}dtt\min\left\{2\pi|k|\delta\sqrt{\delta}t,c^{\prime\prime}\right\}^{2}|A^{k}(\theta)|

\displaystyle\geq\frac{72c^{\prime\prime}(1-3\delta)}{\pi^{4}}\int_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}dtt\min\left\{2\pi|k|\delta\sqrt{\delta}t,c^{\prime\prime}\right\}^{2}\textrm{ by \eqref{misuraAkkvperp=0} and \eqref{misuraAtheta}}

\displaystyle=\frac{72c^{\prime\prime}(1-3\delta)}{\pi^{4}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcr}\frac{(\pi\delta\sqrt{\delta})^{2}|k|^{2}}{16},&&|k|\leq\frac{c^{\prime\prime}}{\pi\delta\sqrt{\delta}},\\
\frac{c^{\prime\prime 4}}{(4\pi\delta\sqrt{\delta}|k|)^{2}}+\frac{c^{\prime\prime 2}}{8}(1-\frac{c^{\prime\prime 2}}{(\pi\delta\sqrt{\delta}|k|)^{2}}),&&|k|\geq\frac{c^{\prime\prime}}{\pi\delta\sqrt{\delta}},\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\geq C\min\{1,|k|^{2}\},

up to another constant
C\in(0,1)
not depending on
\theta
or
h
. Therefore property (
2.3.11
) is proved.
∎

3.
Existence and uniqueness in
L^{p}
.

In this Section we focus on the existence and the uniqueness of the mild solution of the three equations (
1.1.2
), (
1.1.2
) and (
1.1.2
), meaning (
1.1.9
), (
1.1.12
) and (
1.1.15
) respectively, as in Definition
1.3
. The main result we need is the following.

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
\mathbb{X}=\mathbb{R}^{2}
or
\mathbb{X}=\mathbb{T}^{2}=\mathbb{R}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}^{2}
,
p\in[1,+\infty]
and
\mu_{0}\in\cap_{T>0}L^{p}(\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}\times[0,T]\times[-1,1])
. Then the mild solution of the equation (
1.1.2
) is unique in the class
\cap_{T>0}L^{p}(\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}\times[0,T]\times[-1,1])
. Moreover for any
T>0
and at any time
t>0
it holds

(3.0.1)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{X}}dx\int_{\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}}d\theta\int_{0}^{T}ds\int_{-1}^{1}dh|\mu_{t}(x,\theta,s,h)|\leq\int_{\mathbb{X}}dx\int_{\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}}d\theta\int_{0}^{T+t}ds\int_{-1}^{1}dh|\mu_{0}(x,\theta,s,h)|,

and if
\mu_{0}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}\times[0,+\infty)\times[-1,1])
then also
\mu_{t}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}\times[0,+\infty)\times[-1,1])
and the following properties hold

(3.0.2)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{X}}dx\int_{\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}}d\theta\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\int_{-1}^{1}dh|\mu_{t}(x,\theta,s,h)|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{X}}dx\int_{\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}}d\theta\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\int_{-1}^{1}dh|\mu_{0}(x,\theta,s,h)|,

(3.0.3)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{X}}dx\int_{\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}}d\theta\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\int_{-1}^{1}dh\mu_{t}(x,\theta,s,h)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{X}}dx\int_{\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}}d\theta\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\int_{-1}^{1}dh\mu_{0}(x,\theta,s,h).

Finally it holds

\displaystyle\|\mu_{t}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}\times[0,T]\times[-1,1])}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\mu_{0}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}\times[0,t+T]\times[-1,1])}

(3.0.4)

\displaystyle+C\int_{\mathbb{T}^{1}_{2\pi}}d\theta^{\prime}\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}\int_{-1}^{1}dh^{\prime}\|\mu_{0}(\cdot,\theta^{\prime},t^{\prime},h^{\prime})\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{X})},

and therefore, if
